I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I cry over little things, like those potluck lists, and I get worked up about calls I used to handle easily. I’ve even missed some church planning things I normally never would’ve missed. So… I suppose sad or down is fair to say.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness or low mood is present for most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it’s been there most days. I don’t want to overstate it, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I cry over things like the potluck lists, and calls I used to handle easily get me all worked up. And I’ve started missing church planning things I really never would’ve missed before. So I guess the low mood is there a lot, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like choir or church events, no longer give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they give me no pleasure anymore. I mean, I still care about choir and church things… they’ve always meant a lot to me. Maybe it doesn’t feel quite the same right now, but I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it. I suppose I’m having a harder time getting the usual satisfaction from it, but I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like choir or church events, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I no longer take pleasure in them, exactly. That sounds a bit stronger than what I mean. I still do care about choir and church things, and sometimes it’s still nice to be there. It’s just… maybe it doesn’t feel as easy or as full as it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in choir or church events, or that you no longer care about them the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest? I suppose I’m having a harder time getting the usual satisfaction from it, but I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like choir or church events, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I no longer take pleasure in them, exactly. That sounds a bit stronger than what I mean. I still do care about choir and church things, and sometimes it’s still nice to be there. It’s just… maybe it doesn’t feel as easy or as full as it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in choir or church events, or that you no longer care about them the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest? No, I don’t think that’s right. I’d feel bad saying that, because I do still care about choir and the church events. They’re important to me. It’s just… I may not feel as lively about them right now, or it takes more effort, but I haven’t stopped caring. Is that distinction okay?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that being in the choir or attending church events feels like a chore now, or that you don’t get any joy from them at all, even when you’re there?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t call it a chore, no… at least not exactly. And I don’t think it’s true that I get no joy from it at all. When I’m there, there are still moments that feel nice or comforting. Maybe I’m not as uplifted as I used to be, but I don’t want to overstate it. I still care about being part of it. Is that answering what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been a little off lately. Ever since a friend from my choir passed away so suddenly, I haven’t quite been myself. I’ve even missed a couple of church planning things, which isn’t like me at all. Is that… the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like choir or church events, don’t interest you as much anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about choir and the church things… I suppose I just haven’t felt quite as eager to go sometimes. But once I’m there, I do try to participate. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment out of choir or church events as you used to, even when you do go?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t want to say I don’t enjoy them. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day… that sounds a little strong, maybe. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I cry over things that shouldn’t set me off, like potluck lists, and I get all worked up over calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… yes, something’s been weighing on me, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, your mood has been down or sad since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Most days… yes, I think my mood has been lower than usual. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I cry over little things, like those potluck lists, and I get worked up about calls I used to handle easily. I’ve even missed some church planning things I normally never would’ve missed. So… I suppose sad or down is fair to say.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness or low mood is present for most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it’s been there most days. I don’t want to overstate it, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day… that sounds a little strong, maybe. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I cry over things that shouldn’t set me off, like potluck lists, and I get all worked up over calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… yes, something’s been weighing on me, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, your mood has been down or sad since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Most days… yes, I think my mood has been lower than usual. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I cry over little things, like those potluck lists, and I get worked up about calls I used to handle easily. I’ve even missed some church planning things I normally never would’ve missed. So… I suppose sad or down is fair to say.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness or low mood is present for most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it’s been there most days. I don’t want to overstate it, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I suppose I’m having a harder time getting the usual satisfaction from it, but I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like choir or church events, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I no longer take pleasure in them, exactly. It’s just… maybe it doesn’t feel as easy or as full as it used to. I know people go through these things, and I don’t want to make too much of it, but I find myself crying over silly things, like the potluck lists, and getting all worked up over phone calls I used to handle just fine. I’ve even missed a couple of church planning things, which isn’t like me at all. Is that… the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like choir or church events, don’t interest you as much anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still care about choir and the church things… I suppose I just haven’t felt quite as eager to go sometimes. But once I’m there, I do try to participate. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment out of choir or church events as you used to, even when you do go?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t want to say I don’t enjoy them. I still like seeing people and hearing the music. Maybe it feels a little flatter than it used to, but I do still get something out of it when I’m there. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day, nearly every day… that sounds a little strong, maybe. But since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I really haven’t felt like myself. I cry over things that shouldn’t set me off, like potluck lists, and I get all worked up over calls I used to handle just fine. And I’ve missed some church planning things I never would’ve missed before. So… yes, something’s been weighing on me, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, your mood has been down or sad since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Most days… yes, I think my mood has been lower than usual. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I cry over little things, like those potluck lists, and I get worked up about calls I used to handle easily. I’ve even missed some church planning things I normally never would’ve missed. So… I suppose sad or down is fair to say.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness or low mood is present for most of the day, nearly every day, since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it’s been there most days. I don’t want to overstate it, but ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself. I cry over things like the potluck lists, and calls I used to handle easily get me all worked up. And I’ve started missing church planning things I really never would’ve missed before. I mean, I still care about choir and church things… they’ve always meant a lot to me. Maybe it doesn’t feel quite the same right now, but I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it. I suppose I’m having a harder time getting the usual satisfaction from it, but I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like choir or church events, even when you do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I no longer take pleasure in them, exactly. That sounds a bit stronger than what I mean. I still do care about choir and church things, and sometimes it’s still nice to be there. It’s just… maybe it doesn’t feel as easy or as full as it used to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in choir or church events, or that you no longer care about them the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest? No, I don’t think that’s right. I’d feel bad saying that, because I do still care about choir and the church events. They’re important to me. Is that distinction okay?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that being in the choir or attending church events feels like a chore now, or that you don’t get any joy from them at all, even when you’re there?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t call it a chore, no… at least not exactly. Maybe I’m not as uplifted as I used to be, but I don’t want to overstate it. I still care about being part of it.
